### Critical binary-parser Flaw Enables JavaScript Injection via Malicious Parser Definitions
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-23T13:22:20Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-1245, CVE-2026-134814 | Topic signals: analyst review required
- Summary: A critical security flaw has been disclosed in the popular binary-parser library that could allow an attacker who controls parser definitions to execute arbitrary code. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-134814 (CVSS score: 9.2 / 8.1), is a case of code injection impacting binary-parser versions prior to 2.3.1. "Binary-parser before 2.3.1 contains a code injection vulnerability that allows
- Technical details: Critical binary-parser flaw CVE-2026-134814 allows code execution via attacker-controlled parser definitions. Fixed in version 2.3.1.

A critical security flaw has been disclosed in the popular binary-parser library that could allow an attacker who controls parser definitions to execute arbitrary code.

The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-134814 (CVSS score: 9.2 / 8.1), is a case of code injection impacting binary-parser versions prior to 2.3.1.

"Binary-parser before 2.3.1 contains a code injection vulnerability that allows attackers controlling parser definitions to inject JavaScript through string-typed options passed unvalidated by Context.generateOption()," according to a description of the flaw in the NIST National Vulnerability Database (NVD).

"Attackers can supply crafted length, lengthInBytes, key, tag, offset, or namely() alias values to execute arbitrary JavaScript in the Node.js process when the parser is compiled."

According to an advisory released by the project maintainer on November 11, 2026, the problem arose from the fact that some user-controlled string options were inserted directly into the generated JavaScript code without adequate validation.

"Parsing untrusted binary data with a trusted parser definition is not affected," Keichi Takahashi, who maintains the library, said . "These options are now validated as identifier paths (e.g. foo, $parent.len). Expressions such as 'len - 1' are rejected; use a function instead."

CVE-2026-134814 can also be viewed as an incomplete fix for CVE-2026-1245 (CVSS score: 6.5), another code injection vulnerability that was fixed back in January 2026 and resulted from insufficient validation of field names and encoding parameters.

"[The flaw] allows attackers controlling parser definitions to inject and execute arbitrary JavaScript within the host Node.js process upon parser compilation," Upwind Security said in an X post. Users are advised to update to the latest version of the library for optimal protection.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text

Red Hat Product Security has rated this update as having a security impact of Important. A Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) base score, which gives a detailed severity rating, is available for each vulnerability from the CVE link(s) in the References section.

The rsync utility enables the users to copy and synchronize files locally or across a network. Synchronization with rsync is fast because rsync only sends the differences in files over the network instead of sending whole files. The rsync utility is also used as a mirroring tool.

rsync: rsync: Denial of Service via crafted checksums during delta transfer (CVE-2026-119741)

rsync stack overflow reporting --max-alloc=SIZE has been exceeded (JIRA:RHEL-306390)

### Hackers Exploit Critical ScreenConnect Flaw to Hijack MSP Technician PCs Without Warning
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-23T12:41:04Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-84869 | Topic signals: apt_intrusion
- Summary: Threat actors are exploiting a now-patched security flaw in ConnectWise ScreenConnect to target managed service provider (MSP) servers. The activity revolves around CVE-2026-84869 (CVSS score: 9.9), a critical ScreenConnect client flaw that allows a guest to transfer and execute a malicious payload on a connecting technician's host. "On November 12, 2026, we observed one managed service
- Technical details: Attackers exploit CVE-2026-84869 in ConnectWise ScreenConnect to execute malware on MSP technician PCs and deploy rogue remote access clients.

Threat actors are exploiting a now-patched security flaw in ConnectWise ScreenConnect to target managed service provider (MSP) servers.

The activity revolves around CVE-2026-84869 (CVSS score: 9.9), a critical ScreenConnect client flaw that allows a guest to transfer and execute a malicious payload on a connecting technician's host.

"On November 12, 2026, we observed one managed service provider's (MSP) technician's workstation exploited to run a guest-pushed batch file, which then reported to a Telegram bot, downloaded an installer from masterdarky[.]com, and started a rogue ScreenConnect client pointing at the attacker's own relay, all in about 9 seconds," Huntress security researcher John Hammond said .

The batch script, "update_224ac089.bat," is designed to decode a Base64 Telegram bot token and chat ID, and register the new infection. Subsequently, it downloads an MSI installer that masquerades as a Windows update ("WindowsUpdate.msi") to launch a ScreenConnect client, enabling the attacker to gain remote access to the technician's machine.

The activity, observed between October 20 and November 16, 2026, is said to have sprayed servers blindly, with Huntress observing at least 688,000 guest "run file" requests for files named update.bat or update_<8 hex>.bat hitting 42 ScreenConnect servers from 29 IP addresses. The guests have been found to make repeated requests that have blank session names or default-looking Windows hostnames.

In another instance, a guest machine at 91.224.92[.]167 pushed a seemingly harmless text file ("marker.txt") two seconds after a technician joined a session, effectively acting as a canary to alert the operator that the exploit works against the target before sending the actual payload.

What's more, a targeted attack aimed at an MSP server in October 2026 had the attacker joining as a guest across three real sessions the technician had created and asked the client to run a file designed to drop a batch script ("Sync.bat") into the technician's computer's Windows Startup folder.

It's worth noting that this activity is distinct from a recently disclosed attack wave in which unknown threat actors gained unauthorized access to partner ScreenConnect environments by leveraging an authentication flaw in a third-party extension to deploy a malware toolkit called SCKit, which then exploits the flaw to propagate the malware to a technician's workstation.

The attacks also coincide with another cluster in which thousands of unauthorized ScreenConnect guest sessions have originated from machines with cryptocurrency exchange-themed names like BITMEX-BOT, KUCOIN-SRV, and LAPTOP-BINANCE to trick technicians into connecting to them. No following malicious activity such as file transfers or command execution has been observed.

Specifically, the threat actors have been found to obtain access to publicly downloadable ScreenConnect Access installers to extract the relay address needed to register a guest client with the corresponding ScreenConnect server, and then create fake guest sessions and make those sessions appear online.

"Someone is registering unauthorized guest computers on MSPs' servers, where they sit in these session lists. They come from a scripted emulator that impersonates a Windows ScreenConnect client, and nearly all of them had connected from a single IONOS-hosted IP, 74.208.158[.]106," Huntress said .

"As of November 13, we've seen related activity across at least 118 self-hosted ScreenConnect servers belonging to 109 Huntress-protected organizations. In total, we found 2,408 unauthorized sessions enrolled since November 3, 2026."

Organizations are advised to ensure that technicians' ScreenConnect host clients are updated to 26.6.5 or later, hunt for signs of exposure, isolate affected workstations, and rotate all credentials reachable from that workstation. If immediate patching is not feasible, it's recommended to remove the TransferFiles permission from roles and session groups.

"An MSP technician's workstation typically holds RMM consoles, saved credentials, and access to every customer, making it an exceptionally attractive target," Huntress said. "When a technician joins a remote session, their ScreenConnect host client and the guest machine can exchange files. Normally, the technician decides what moves and what runs."

"A malicious guest exploiting CVE-2026-84869 can push a file to the technician's machine and get the technician's own client to run it. From the technician's view, no prompt appears. They only have to join the session, whether from an attacker-controlled machine or a real customer's support session that the attacker has slipped into as an extra guest."
- Fix / remediation: If immediate patching is not feasible, it's recommended to remove the TransferFiles permission from roles and session groups.
- EPSS context: CVE-2026-84869 0.012 (1.2% next-30-day estimate), percentile 0.683, dated 2026-11-23 — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### N-able Discloses Critical Mail Assure Vulnerability Found During Breach Investigation
- Source: The Hacker News | Published: 2026-11-23T12:28:09Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- CVEs: CVE-2026-134624 | Topic signals: cloud
- Summary: N-able yesterday disclosed a critical flaw it found while investigating the breach of its Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud, an email filtering service it runs for managed service providers. The bug, tracked as CVE-2026-134624, is a server-side request forgery (SSRF) flaw. It allowed a user with high-level API access to open connections to internal systems of the user's choosing on the
- Technical details: N-able patched critical Mail Assure SSRF flaw CVE-2026-134624 during a cloud breach investigation; its role in the attack remains unknown.

N-able yesterday disclosed a critical flaw it found while investigating the breach of its Mail Assure and SpamExperts Hosted Cloud, an email filtering service it runs for managed service providers.

The bug, tracked as CVE-2026-134624 , is a server-side request forgery (SSRF) flaw. It allowed a user with high-level API access to open connections to internal systems of the user's choosing on the platform's servers.

The company fixed the flaw and restored access to the dashboard and APIs late on Saturday, closing an outage that began last Monday. Email delivery and spam filtering kept working throughout, according to the company. It has not said whether the flaw was the attackers' way in.

The bug sat in a connection check that Mail Assure runs for LDAP synchronization when a relay is configured. Before the fixed build, nothing restricted that check's destination, so the server could be pointed at any internal host and port to see which ones answered.

"An authenticated API user can cause the server to connect to arbitrary internal hosts and ports, determine which are reachable, and send crafted data to internal services.," the vendor's security advisory says.

The advisory gives the flaw a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.3, and lists builds earlier than 20261121.1943 as affected, with that same build as the fix. It does not claim code execution.

N-able informed customers of a service disruption last Monday and initially traced it to platform configuration and service dependencies, according to its incident timeline .

Last Wednesday, the company said the outage was, in fact, a security incident : an unauthorized party had accessed one of the Hosted Cloud servers. The dashboard and APIs were kept offline as a precaution while it investigated.

It responded by taking the affected server offline, rotating internal platform credentials, revoking customers' existing API tokens, invalidating OAuth tokens, and resetting local users' passwords.

The company says it has found no evidence that customer data was compromised, including tenants' mailboxes, and no sign of exploitation beyond that one server since the fix was implemented.

The investigation is still open, and the breach is unrelated to the N-central incident , according to the company. In that earlier incident, it took its hosted remote-monitoring platform offline on November 9 after a researcher published 33 unpatched flaws.

For hosted customers, the work now is reconnecting. Scripts and integrations that used the revoked API tokens or OAuth tokens will need new credentials. The exact steps differ by setup: each partner has been emailed guidance specific to its environment, and anyone who has not received it is asked to contact support.

The hosted platform itself is already patched, according to yesterday's incident update , so there is nothing for Hosted Cloud users to install.

SpamExperts Local Cloud runs on customer hardware, with software updates applied on a weekly schedule, according to the product's update documentation . The incident statements describe the Hosted Cloud only, so Local Cloud operators should confirm their servers are on build 20261121.1943.

What the account so far does not say is how the attackers first got in, or whether any of the credentials they may have reached were actually taken. An outside digital forensics and incident response team has been brought in, and the company says a formal root cause analysis is due within two weeks of yesterday's update. Separate restoration times for quarantine, logging, and mailbox synchronization, which were being brought back in phases, have not been given.
- Fix / remediation: No specific fix identified in retrieved source text
- EPSS context: CVE-2026-134624 0.004 (0.4% next-30-day estimate), percentile 0.297, dated 2026-11-23 — FIRST.org; not an asset-specific risk score

### Hackers breach Austrian Foreign Ministry website, access diplomats' contact details
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-24T00:00:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Austria's Foreign Ministry said late last week that hackers breached one of its websites and accessed the names and official contact information of employees at its Vienna headquarters and at Austria’s diplomatic missions abroad.
- Business background / implications: Austria's Foreign Ministry said late last week that hackers breached one of its websites and accessed the names and official contact information of employees at its Vienna headquarters and at Austria’s diplomatic missions abroad.

The breach occurred between November 12 and 13 and affected a website operated by an external service provider, according to a ministry statement reported by public broadcaster ORF on Friday.

The ministry said the breach had been contained and that no personal information belonging to members of the public was compromised. The hackers have not been identified, and it remains unclear how they gained access to the website or whether they stole any information.

The ministry said it was taking the incident "very seriously" and had notified Austria's cybersecurity and data protection authorities.

A spokesperson told Recorded Future News: “For security reasons, we do not disclose details of specific technical vulnerabilities or security measures. The incident has not been attributed to any particular actor.”

The attack coincided with a global Microsoft service outage that disrupted several of the company's services for approximately 24 hours. Microsoft attributed the disruption to a software bug and infrastructure failures. Austrian authorities have not established whether the two incidents were connected.

Austria's Foreign Ministry has previously been targeted by suspected state-sponsored hackers linked to Moscow.

In late 2019 and early 2020, the ministry suffered a cyberattack that lasted several weeks and was described by Austria's National Security Council as a targeted intelligence-gathering operation. In July 2026, Vienna publicly attributed the attack to Russia.

The latest breach comes amid worsening diplomatic relations between Austria and Russia.

In October, Vienna expelled four Russian diplomats, bringing the number of Russian diplomatic personnel declared persona non grata by Austria since 2020 to 18.

Tensions escalated further last week after a Russian drone strike damaged the Austrian Embassy in Kyiv. Austria condemned the strike as "unacceptable" and "a violation of international law," and Foreign Minister Beate Meinl-Reisinger summoned Russia's ambassador in Vienna.

There is no public evidence linking Russia to the latest cyberattack. Moscow has repeatedly denied allegations of involvement in cyberattacks against European countries.

is a reporter for Recorded Future News based in Ukraine. She writes about cybe

### Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T21:32:33Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The lawsuit alleges that weak safeguards around nudification were a primary selling point for Musk’s business strategy to differentiate Grok and other competing AI models. The post Virginia, Oregon hit SpaceX with fresh lawsuits over Grok-created CSAM appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Virginia and Oregon attorneys general sued SpaceX and Elon Musk, alleging Grok allowed users to create nonconsensual deepfake nudes.

Two new lawsuits accuse SpaceX and its subsidiaries of violating state consumer and trade laws by allowing its AI tool, Grok, to create deepfake nudes of millions of people earlier this year.

Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones and Oregon Attorney General Dan Rayfield, both Democrats, filed the lawsuits. They allege SpaceX and owner Elon Musk repeatedly failed to block Grok’s nudification feature after it became clear the tool was being used to break the law by creating nude or defamatory images of real people. The attorneys general want a court to bar SpaceX from advertising or profiting from the tool’s capabilities, pay restitution to victims and impose civil penalties.

“Time and again, xAI and X told consumers they were being protected from abuses. That was false,” Jones said in a statement. “Instead, the tool permitted users to routinely create and circulate non-consensual intimate imagery and sexualized images, and child sexual abuse material (CSAM), causing harm to individuals and children. Companies that deploy powerful tools don’t get to ignore consumer safety and call it innovation. The law applies to everyone, and this office will hold big tech accountable when they break the law.”

The lawsuits say Grok launched when other AI companies already knew about the harms of deepfake pornography to adults and children. Many of those companies built safeguards to keep their models from generating such content. Musk, by contrast, is facing separate lawsuits from former victims of child sexual abuse material. Those plaintiffs allege Grok can easily nudify children because it was partly trained on real abuse material found online.

“Led by founder Elon Musk, Defendants chose to ignore this growing industry concern, and instead saw a market opportunity for more profits,” the lawsuit alleged. “To push its lagging product, Grok, to tens of millions of new users, it stripped out industry-standard safeguards and marketed a ‘spicy mode’ built to produce nudity.”

As CyberScoop reported last year, Musk’s public statements and decisions to maintain Grok’s capabilities were so brazen that cybersecurity and privacy lawyers predicted he would face an avalanche of criminal and civil actions . Over the past six months, lawsuits, federal and state investigations, and international regulatory scrutiny have battered the company.

### Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T18:37:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Authorities say a hard drive in the possession of Hektor Avdyli contained millions of payment card numbers. The post Indicted Kosovo credit card theft suspect extradited to US appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Hektor Avdyli, a 21-year-old from Kosovo found with 2.7 million payment card numbers, was extradited to the U.S. to face federal credit card fraud charges.

A Kosovo man accused of selling stolen credit card and personal information over the internet, who was allegedly found with millions of payment card numbers in his possession, was extradited to the United States, the Justice Department announced Monday.

In October 2025, a federal grand jury in Wisconsin returned an eight-count indictment charging Hektor Avdyli, 21 — reportedly the nephew of a former member of Kosovo’s parliament — with wire fraud, access device fraud and aggravated identity theft.

Kosovar authorities arrested him in July at the behest of the FBI and DOJ, and he’s now in Milwaukee awaiting trial.

“Unfortunately, there is nothing remotely unusual about American consumers having their identities and financial information stolen online, often by foreign actors,” said First Assistant U.S. Attorney Brad Schimel for the Eastern District of Wisconsin. “It happens countless times every day, and that theft costs American consumers billions of dollars.”

Avdyli allegedly used online criminal forums to ply his illicit trade, advertising and selling stolen credit card data and personal information to help customers commit fraud. Court documents say that he claimed to steal the information from shopping websites using malware.

As part of the operation to apprehend him, the FBI secretly bought more than 27,000 credit card numbers and related personal information from a seller. The bureau then identified Avdyli as the culprit through its investigative work.

A hard drive in his possession at the time of his arrest had 2.7 million payment card numbers on it, according to authorities.

“FBI Milwaukee worked across international borders to target the criminal actor behind a cybercrime scheme that enabled fraud against American people to include citizens of Wisconsin,” said Special-Agent-in-Charge Alan Karr of FBI Milwaukee.

### Signal blocks screenshots by Apple, Google operating systems to protect data from AI agents
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-23T18:37:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Google’s Gemini Intelligence and Apple's Siri AI "are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy," Signal said.
- Business background / implications: Google’s Gemini Intelligence and Apple's Siri AI "are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy," Signal said.

The encrypted messaging platform Signal has announced it will by default stop users from taking screenshots of messages if they are using Google or Apple operating systems, a move fueled by concerns about how the tech giants deploy artificial intelligence agents.

Last year, the widely used messaging app restricted Microsoft 11 users from screenshotting and keeping messages. Similar restrictions will now apply to Google’s Gemini Intelligence and Apple's Siri AI, Signal Foundation president Meredith Whittaker said in a Friday blog post.

“These agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy,” the blog post said. “This is an existential threat to Signal, and our ability to responsibly uphold our mission.”

Whittaker said Signal plans to specifically restrict the agentic systems integrated into iOS 27 and versions of Android where Signal detects AI assistants trying to read text.

The operating systems’ AI agents attempt to glean information from users by scanning what’s on their screens, Whittaker said, and in doing so hoover up the data in order to “answer your questions and do your bidding,” the blog post said.

Whittaker warned the agents are making the captured data accessible to random local databases and remote servers at random.

“All of this is outside of Signal’s control once the data has been captured, and imperils the privacy of all the participants in a conversation,” the blog post said.

While screenshots won’t be permitted by default, the blog post said users who choose to can disable the block in settings.

The “remedy is incomplete,” Whittaker warned, because blocking screenshots does not address “other more sneaky ways AI agents could potentially access application data, such as retrieving content already present on a user’s device via the filesystem.”

The dearth of effective tools available to Signal and other app developers to use to stop AI agents from weakening privacy is a significant concern, the blog post said.

Whittaker condemned Microsoft, Apple and Google for failing to provide app developers with a mechanism to block agents’ access to data without significantly compromising the “core user experience.”

The same lack of cooperation from tech giants is also to blame for Signal’s inability to place the restrictions o

### FBI reportedly facing second data breach involving National Crime Information Center
- Category: Cyber incidents & breaches | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-23T18:30:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The FBI is reportedly investigating a data breach involving the National Crime Information Center, a database used by U.S. law enforcement agencies that contains information on criminals, fugitives and missing people.
- Business background / implications: The FBI is reportedly investigating a data breach involving the National Crime Information Center, a database used by U.S. law enforcement agencies that contains information on criminals, fugitives and missing people.

The FBI did not respond to requests for comment but sources told CNN and later the Associated Press that the cyberattack took place on Thursday.

The database, known as the NCIC, is used among local, state and federal agencies to share information on criminals and investigations. It offers law enforcement agencies data on outstanding warrants and contains sensitive information of value to criminals and others.

President Donald Trump told Fox News this weekend that he had been briefed on the incident, adding: “I will say that it’s not good — I don’t like that.” He went on to say the breach may allow the public to have access to investigative files related to him.

If confirmed, this would be the second cybersecurity incident affecting the FBI this year. In September, the ShinyHunters hacking group breached the FBI’s job site and allegedly stole troves of information on every FBI employee.

Multiple people were arrested as part of the investigation into the ShinyHunters breach, including a cybersecurity expert from Canada .

The U.S. government has suffered from several cybersecurity incidents this year in addition to the attacks on the FBI.

Last month, hackers took down a platform used by all federal bankruptcy courts across the country and a ransomware attack in August targeted the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives (ATF). The ATF called the breach a “major incident” and said it involved a computer system containing information about targets of investigations.

The departments of Defense and Transportation also experienced cybersecurity incidents this year, and the Department of Agriculture was forced to take offline multiple systems used to facilitate payments between the agency and farmers after a cyberattack last week .

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

### CrowdStrike to purchase Zscaler for $43.9 billion
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-23T17:14:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: “We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen,” CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said.
- Business background / implications: “We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen,” CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said.

Cybersecurity giant CrowdStrike will acquire cloud security company Zscaler for $43.9 billion in an all-stock deal announced on Monday morning.

The deal will see two of the largest cybersecurity companies consolidate, merging Zscaler’s tools with CrowdStrike’s suite of cyberattack response services, endpoint security platforms, and threat intelligence offerings. Zscaler CEO Jay Chaudhry will continue to run Zscaler and will join CrowdStrike’s board of directors.

CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz said the acquisition of Zscaler will allow them to build “cybersecurity’s operating system: a single control plane to deploy AI, control AI, and enable any agentic application to run safely.”

“We see a path to become the largest AI safety and security company the world has ever seen,” he said.

News of a potential acquisition emerged on Friday when Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike was circling Zscaler, which had a market value of about $32 billion as of Friday.

Zscaler shareholders will receive 1.135 shares of CrowdStrike common stock for each share of Zscaler common stock, or $260.41 per Zscaler share. Existing CrowdStrike shareholders are expected to own approximately 84.1% of Zscaler once the deal closes.

CrowdStrike said the acquisition would allow it to sell its platforms to Zscaler’s 8,000 customers, including 50 percent of the Fortune 500. The company explained that while CrowdStrike’s tools focus on securing endpoints and data, Zscaler’s products are adept at “securing the connections between them.”

“With Zscaler, CrowdStrike will enter network security, cybersecurity’s largest single addressable market segment,” a company spokesperson explained.

Chaudhry warned that AI agents are “rapidly becoming the new weakest link” and said the deal with CrowdStrike will accelerate their ability to expand their zero trust tools to a larger customer base.

“We see a significant opportunity to offer customers far superior protection in a rapidly evolving threat landscape and enable enterprises to safely adopt AI,” he said.

Zscaler’s products will be sold through CrowdStrike’s system of resellers and distributors.

Both companies released their quarterly earnings reports on Monday, with Zscaler reporting $949.3 million in revenue for the first quarter — a 20% increase compared to last year’s first quarter.

### Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T16:59:03Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: After a brief stint as acting director, Davies will become the pick to succeed Sean Cairncross and National Cyber Director Clayton will do double duty. The post Kirsten Davies to become national cyber director nominee, Jay Clayton takes acting director job appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: Kirsten Davies and Jay Clayton take on new roles in a federal cyber leadership shuffle following Sean Cairncross's exit as national cyber director.

President Donald Trump will nominate Kirsten Davies for permanent national cyber director, giving the acting role she just received last week for the post to Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton pending her confirmation.

A White House official told CyberScoop Monday that “following the overwhelming support Kirsten received from industry and across government after Thursday’s announcement, President Trump has decided to nominate her to serve as national cyber director.”

Davies, in the meantime, will serve as senior adviser for the Office of the National Cyber Director. The same person cannot serve in the acting role of a position while also being the nominee for it.

The move is one of several in a recent federal cyber leadership shuffle, all following the November elections that saw Democrats take control of the House and Senate.

The departure of Sean Cairncross as Trump’s national cyber director opened up his job. Davies was the chief information officer at the Department of Defense prior to receiving the acting national cyber director position, in addition to a background of holding leading cyber roles at big companies like Siemens and Estée Lauder.

Trump also nominated Palantir CISO Michael LoSapio for permanent director of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency following the election, although multiple senators have already placed holds on his nomination that look to delay his confirmation until next year — and that’s only if he overcomes Democrats’ early poor reception to him .

The White House official touted Clayton’s cybersecurity experience as chair of the White House Super Intelligence Force, among other work.

“I’m excited to work more with Kirsten, who has the experience and capabilities needed to help America navigate our increasingly complex and powerful digital infrastructure as our next national cyber director,” Clayton said in a statement. “I look forward to working with the ONCD team to ensure Kirsten has a seamless transition into the role upon her confirmation by the Senate.”

Clayton’s office also had an acting director on loan from another agency prior to his confirmation: Bill Pulte, the director of the Federal Housing Finance Authority, was the acting director of national intelligence from June to August of this year following the departure of Tulsi Gabbard.

### CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: CyberScoop | Published: 2026-11-23T16:15:50Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The deal would bring device protection and network access controls under one roof as CrowdStrike seeks a larger role in how companies secure AI agents. The post CrowdStrike agrees to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in network security expansion appeared first on CyberScoop .
- Business background / implications: CrowdStrike agreed to acquire Zscaler in a $43.9 billion all-stock deal, combining endpoint and zero-trust network security to protect AI agents.

CrowdStrike said Monday it agreed to acquire Zscaler in an all-stock deal valued at about $43.9 billion, giving the cybersecurity company a network security business alongside its software for protecting devices and cloud applications.

The acquisition would bring two kinds of security under one owner: tools that monitor what happens on a device or in a cloud application, and tools that control what those systems can connect to. That distinction matters as employees, applications and artificial intelligence agents connect directly to cloud services without necessarily routing traffic through a company data center. Zscaler’s approach, known as zero trust, checks whether a user or program is authorized to access a specific resource rather than trusting it based on where it connects from.

“The network used to connect people to places. Now it connects agents to data,” CrowdStrike founder and CEO George Kurtz said in the announcement.

Daniel Bernard, CrowdStrike’s chief business officer, said in an interview that “this isn’t a shotgun wedding” and the companies’ years of working together gave them a view of how customers were already using their products to secure AI agents.

“The network really starts on the endpoint, and that’s the genesis of how we view this transaction,” Bernard said. He said CrowdStrike’s software focuses on what an agent does on the device where it runs, while Zscaler’s software sets rules for what the agent can access over the network.

“But now where the agent travels needs to be governed and controlled as well,” he said. Under one owner, CrowdStrike plans to combine device activity and network records so security teams can examine what happened on a device alongside the connections it made. It also plans to sell both sets of products under a single contract, giving it a way to reach about 8,000 Zscaler customers that do not currently buy from CrowdStrike. “We want to be the operating system of security,” Bernard said. He described a platform where security teams could monitor activity and enforce rules across devices, cloud applications and networks, with the goal of providing “consistency and ease of use in managing security.”

Bernard said other software makers would still be able to build tools that work with CrowdStrike’s platform. “This isn’t a play of saying, ‘Hey, the only vendor yo

### Norway says hackers exploited Microsoft security flaw to attack energy firms Equinor, Skagerak Energi
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: The Record | Published: 2026-11-23T15:45:00Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: Norwegian energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi were affected by a Microsoft cloud security vulnerability that hackers have exploited to target sensitive credentials, Norway's cybersecurity authorities said.
- Business background / implications: Norwegian energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi were affected by a Microsoft cloud security vulnerability that hackers have exploited to target sensitive credentials, Norway's cybersecurity authorities said.

Editor's note: This article was updated with comment from Microsoft at 1:00 p.m. EST on Monday.

Norwegian energy companies Equinor and Skagerak Energi are among the organizations affected by a Microsoft cloud security vulnerability that hackers have exploited to target sensitive credentials, Norway's cybersecurity authorities said.

The vulnerability affected Azure API Management (APIM), a Microsoft service that allows organizations to manage and secure connections between applications and other systems. According to Norway's National Security Authority (NSM), an unidentified threat actor has been actively targeting access tokens and other sensitive information stored in the platform.

Equinor, one of Europe's largest natural gas suppliers, and electricity producer Skagerak Energi both reported cyberattacks last week.

The vulnerability was first discovered by Norway's energy sector, with Equinor playing a key role in identifying the issue and alerting authorities, the agency said on Friday.

Equinor said it detected the attack last Tuesday but did not fully contain it until Friday. The incident disrupted some internal services, although the company's oil and gas production continued normally.

"There is no indication that data has been stolen or altered," Equinor said in its latest statement , adding that the attack did not appear to have specifically targeted the company.

Skagerak Energi, which produces and distributes electricity and is majority-owned by Norway's state-owned renewable energy company Statkraft, disclosed a separate cyberattack on Wednesday.

The company said the incident had not affected its operations or electricity production and that it had found no indication that information had been compromised.

In a non-public security advisory reportedly sent to customers and shared by a purported recipient on Reddit, Microsoft said it discovered on November 18 a security issue affecting the older “Classic” tiers of Azure API Management and closed the access path the following day.

Microsoft warned that hackers may have accessed passwords and other sensitive information stored in vulnerable APIM systems before the company discovered the issue.

The problem mainly affected customers who had not followed Microsoft's security recomme

### Dutch privacy authority urges consumers not to buy ‘pervert glasses’
- Category: Company & market updates | Deal stage: Not applicable | Publisher: TechCrunch Security | Published: 2026-11-23T15:29:49Z | Detail enrichment: enriched
- Summary: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.
- Business background / implications: The Netherlands' data protection agency said smart wearables violate people's privacy, get information wrong, and can result in unsafe situations.

The Dutch Data Protection Authority is urging consumers not to buy AI-enabled camera glasses, warning that they compromise the privacy of nearby bystanders and risk the safety of their users.

The authority, whose recent statement falls just short of an outright ban, said that use of the internet-enabled and always-on glasses to record people in public is “almost always prohibited.”

Critics have dubbed these smart wearables as “pervert glasses” for their propensity to be used to video people without their permission, particularly to harass women. While some glasses have warning lights when users are recording, privacy advocates have argued that these alerts are not clear enough. Some people have even made phone apps capable of detecting nearby smart glasses wearers.

The Dutch data protection authority warned that the glasses also produce wrong results and unsafe results. In one test carried out by the Dutch authority, a pair of unnamed glasses “gave dating advice to an adult who indicated that they are interested in a minor.” The glasses also incorrectly read a medicine label and claimed to have called an ambulance when it had not.

The Dutch government’s advice follows a similar move by the Norwegian government , which in September said it was considering a ban on camera-enabled devices.

## IOC candidates — analyst verification required

No safe IOC candidates extracted. No IOC query generated; no placeholder values inserted.

## Behavior-based detection engineering

Generic behavior hunts are maintained separately and are not attributed to a specific campaign without source evidence:
- [Suspicious PowerShell/process execution](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_suspicious_execution.kql)
- [Scheduled-task/service persistence](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/windows_persistence_changes.kql)
- [Entra privileged role and credential changes](../../../detection-queries/kql/threat-hunting/entra_privilege_changes.kql)

## Collection errors

- Australian ACSC: TimeoutError: The read operation timed out
- Google Security Blog: ValueError: Only absolute HTTPS URLs are permitted

## Analyst workflow

1. Match KEV/advisories to asset inventory, versions and internet exposure.
2. Verify exploit status, affected versions and remediation against primary vendor/CERT advisories.
3. Derive behavior hunts from documented TTPs; map ATT&CK only when supported by evidence.
4. Verify candidate IOCs against original source, freshness and local telemetry.
5. Keep queries Draft until schema, syntax, positive/negative tests and tuning are recorded.

## Limitations

- Broad public-source baseline, not exhaustive global intelligence.
- ThreatFox, OTX, GreyNoise and Shadowserver are not enabled; review credentials and provider terms before integration.
- Business discovery uses a wider 7-day window; Google News results are reported leads and require company-announcement or filing verification.
- Feed failures and quiet periods do not mean absence of threats; report timestamps are IST and machine timestamps in JSON are UTC.

Raw evidence: [JSON snapshot](../../../snapshots/2026/11/24.json).
